Certifiably Optimal Outlier-Robust Geometric Perception: Semidefinite Relaxations and Scalable Global Optimization
Heng Yang, and Luca Carlone

Abstract
We propose the first general and scalable framework to design certifiable algorithms for robust geometric perception in the presence of outliers. Our first contribution is to show that estimation using common robust costs, such as truncated least squares (TLS), maximum consensus, Geman-McClure, Tukey’s biweight, among others, can be reformulated as polynomial optimization problems (POPs). By focusing on the TLS cost, our second contribution is to exploit sparsity in the POP and propose a sparse semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxation that is much smaller than the standard Lasserre’s hierarchy while preserving empirical exactness , i.e. , the SDP recovers the optimizer of the nonconvex POP with an optimality certificate . Our third contribution is to solve the SDP relaxations at an unprecedented scale and accuracy by presenting STRIDE , a solver that blends global descent on the convex SDP with fast local search on the nonconvex POP. Our fourth contribution is an evaluation of the proposed framework on six geometric perception problems including single and multiple rotation averaging, point cloud and mesh registration, absolute pose estimation, and category-level object pose and shape estimation. Our experiments demonstrate that (i) our sparse SDP relaxation is empirically exact with up to 6 ​ 0 % \mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\% – 9 ​ 0 % \mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\% outliers across applications; (ii) while still being far from real-time, STRIDE is up to 1 ​ 0 ​ 0 \mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720 times faster than existing SDP solvers on medium-scale problems, and is the only solver that can solve large-scale SDPs with hundreds of thousands of constraints to high accuracy; (iii) STRIDE safeguards existing fast heuristics for robust estimation ( e.g. , RANSAC or Graduated Non-Convexity), i.e. , it certifies global optimality if the heuristic estimates are optimal, or detects and allows escaping local optima when the heuristic estimates are suboptimal.



\justify

This paper has been accepted for publication in the IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence.

Please cite the paper as: H. Yang and L. Carlone, “Certifiably Optimal Outlier-Robust Geometric Perception:

Semidefinite Relaxations and Scalable Global Optimization”,
IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence
, 2022.

I
Introduction

GEOMETRIC perception, the task of estimating unknown geometric models (
e.g.
, object poses, rotations, 3D structure, robot trajectory) from sensor measurements (
e.g.
, images, point clouds, relative poses), is a fundamental problem in computer vision and robotics. It finds extensive applications to object detection and localization
[
106
]
, motion estimation and 3D reconstruction
[
36
]
, simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM)
[
82
]
and structure from motion (SfM)
[
84
]
,
virtual and augmented reality
[
61
]
, and medical imaging
[
10
]
, to name a few.

A modern machine perception pipeline includes a
perception front-end
that extracts, describes, and matches relevant features from raw sensor data, and a
perception back-end
that estimates the geometric models of interest given the putative feature matches. In practice, due to various sources of imperfections and uncertainties (
e.g.
, sensor failures, incorrect detections and matchings by hand-crafted or deep-learned features), a large amount of
outliers
—measurements that tell no or little information about the underlying geometric models— are generated by the front-end. Therefore, designing an
outlier-robust
back-end that can tolerate large amounts of outliers, also known as
robust fitting

[
35
]
in computer vision and
robust state estimation

[
13
]
in robotics, has been a longstanding quest in both communities.

Unfortunately, from a theoretical standpoint, performing robust estimation by discerning
inliers
(
i.e.
, the correct and useful measurements) from outliers, is known to be NP-hard and
inapproximable
due to its combinatorial nature
[
35
,
8
,
44
,
33
]
. Consequently, existing algorithms for outlier-robust estimation are mostly divided into
fast heuristics
,
e.g.
,
RANSAC

[
47
,
37
,
12
]
and graduated non-convexity (
GNC
)
[
105
,
18
,
19
]
, that are efficient but offer no optimality guarantees, and
global solvers
,
e.g.
, Branch-and-Bound
[
114
]
and mixed-integer programming
[
56
,
69
]
, that guarantee optimality but run in worst-case exponential time. Although in some cases it is acceptable to trade off
optimality
(hence robustness) for
efficiency
, real-time safety-critical applications —such as autonomous driving and space robotics— pose high demands for
efficient global optimality
.

The conflict between the fundamental intractability of robust estimation and the demand for computational efficiency
calls for a paradigm shift: since it is impossible to solve all robust estimation problems in polynomial time, we argue that a useful goal is to design algorithms that perform well in typical instances and are able to
certify
optimality of the resulting estimates, but at the same time can declare “failure” on worst-case instances rather than blindly returning an incorrect estimate. Inspired by related works
[
11
,
113
]
, we formalize the notion of a
certifiable algorithm
below.

Definition 1

(Certifiable Algorithm)
.

Given an optimization problem
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 84054785
with input data
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}
, an algorithm
\mathbb{\mathchar 28993}
is said to be certifiable if (i)
\mathbb{\mathchar 28993}
runs in polynomial time; and after solving
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 84054785
,
\mathbb{\mathchar 28993}
(ii) either returns the global optimizer of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}
together with a certificate of optimality for common instances of
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}
(empirically or provably), or (iii) fails to do so for the worst instances of
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}
but provides a measure of suboptimality (
e.g.
, a bound on the objective value, or the distance to the global optimizer).

A certifiable algorithm respects the theoretical intractability of robust estimation
[
35
,
8
]
in that it does
not
globally optimize
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 84054785
for
all
instances of
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}
and it is allowed to fail in the worst cases (
cf.
(iii) of Definition
1
).
However, our notion of a certifiable algorithm is stricter than that of
[
113
]
, as it requires
\mathbb{\mathchar 28993}
to solve
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 84054785
to global optimality for common
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}
(at least empirically,
cf.
(ii) of Definition
1
). This requirement rules out algorithms that seldomly attain global optimality but provide suboptimality guarantees (
e.g.
, approximation algorithms
[
96
]
).

Semidefinite relaxations
are a natural choice for designing certifiable algorithms. If the problem
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}
is a
polynomial optimization problem
(POP,
i.e.
, both its objective and constraints are polynomials), then there exists a standard semidefinite relaxation
hierarchy
, known as Lasserre’s hierarchy
[
65
]
, that relaxes
\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}
into a hierarchy of convex semidefinite programs (SDPs) of increasing size.
Each relaxation in this hierarchy can be solved in polynomial time
[
93
]
and provides a measure of suboptimality for the resulting estimate.
Moreover, under mild technical conditions,
the suboptimality of these relaxations becomes zero when their size is large enough, in which case we
say the relaxation is
exact
, or
tight
.
1
1

1

Lasserre’s hierarchy respects the worst-case NP-hardness of POPs because one may need an SDP relaxation whose size grows exponentially with the dimension of the POP to attain certifiable optimality.

We provide an accessible introduction to POPs and their relaxations in Section
II
.

Semidefinite relaxations have been successfully used to design certifiable algorithms for many geometric perception problems. The pioneering work by Kahl and Henrion
[
58
]
applies Lasserre’s hierarchy to solve several early perception problems including camera resectioning, homography estimation, and fundamental matrix estimation. Since then, certifiable algorithms have been designed for modern applications such as pose graph optimization
[
29
,
82
]
, rotation averaging
[
45
,
48
]
, triangulation
[
38
,
5
]
, 3D registration
[
24
,
73
,
31
,
55
]
, absolute pose estimation
[
4
]
, relative pose estimation
[
25
,
119
,
49
]
, hand-eye calibration
[
52
,
50
,
102
]
, and category-level object perception
[
108
,
88
]
. Although the original formulations of the problems mentioned above are nonconvex, semidefinite relaxations at the
lowest
relaxation order in the hierarchy are shown to be exact in practical applications. Since the SDP resulting from the lowest relaxation order can usually be solved efficiently (
e.g.
, below one second) by off-the-shelf SDP solvers (
e.g.
,
SDPT3

[
95
]
,
MOSEK

[
9
]
) or the Burer-Monteiro (B-M) low-rank factorization method
[
26
,
22
,
81
]
, both efficiency and (certifiable) optimality can be obtained.

However, these successful examples of certifiable algorithms are underpinned by the restrictive assumption that
the measurements are free of outliers
, which seldomly holds in practice. Heuristics like
RANSAC
and
GNC
are typically used to filter out outliers, but it is precisely the use of such heuristics that breaks the optimality guarantee and makes the system prone to undetected failures. Although several works have attempted to design certifiable algorithms for
outlier-robust
geometric perception
[
100
,
64
,
28
,
107
,
90
]
, most approaches (i) are problem-specific, (ii) cannot tolerate high outlier rates (
e.g.
, above
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
)
[
100
,
64
,
28
]
, or (iii) become too large to be solved by existing SDP solvers
[
107
]
.

Contributions
. In this paper, we propose a general and scalable framework for designing certifiable outlier-robust estimation algorithms that are empirically
exact
with up to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers, and present a fast SDP solver that can solve the tight relaxations at an unprecedented scale. We now describe our four contributions in detail.

(I) Robust estimation as polynomial optimization (Section
III
)
.
We investigate outlier-robust estimation with common robust cost functions, including truncated least squares (TLS), maximum consensus, Geman-McClure, Tukey’s Biweight, L1, Huber, and Barron’s adaptive kernel
[
14
]
. Our first contribution is to show that robust estimation using these costs can be equivalently reformulated as POPs, even though the robust costs themselves are not polynomials. This result is established by introducing additional variables and manipulating the original costs to polynomials.

(II) A sparse, but exact, semidefinite relaxation (Section
IV
)
.
With the POP reformulation, it is tempting to apply the standard Lasserre’s hierarchy to develop certifiable algorithms for robust estimation. Nevertheless, due to the additional variables (one or two variables per measurement), even for small estimation problems with fewer than 20 measurements, the
lowest-order relaxation
can already lead to SDPs that are too large for existing SDP solvers. Therefore, our second contribution is to focus on the TLS cost and show that it allows us to exploit
term sparsity
of the polynomials in the POP and design a much smaller semidefinite relaxation using
basis reduction
. Although exploiting sparsity of POPs is a known idea in applied mathematics
[
99
,
66
]
, our method is more effective than existing generic-purpose techniques since it leverages the special structure of our perception problems. Compared to the standard Lasserre’s hierarchy, our sparse semidefinite relaxation leads to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
times reduction in the size of the SDP. Unfortunately, even with our sparse relaxation, solving the SDP using off-the-shelf SDP solvers (
e.g.
,
MOSEK
) is still too slow, and we can only demonstrate empirical exactness of our relaxation on small estimation problems (
e.g.
,
\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
measurements).

(III) A scalable and robust SDP solver (Section
V
)
.
The limitations of existing SDP solvers lead to our third contribution, a scalable SDP solver that can
certifiably optimally
solve robust estimation problems of moderate but realistic sizes (
e.g.
,
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
measurements). Our solver, called
SpecTrahedral pRojected gradIent Descent along vErtices
(
STRIDE
),
blends fast
local search
on the nonconvex POP with
global descent
on the convex SDP. Specifically,
STRIDE
follows a globally convergent trajectory driven by a
projected gradient descent method
for solving the SDP, while simultaneously probing long, but
safeguarded
,
rank-one
“strides”, generated by fast nonlinear programming algorithms on the POP, to seek rapid descent. Notably, fast heuristics such as
RANSAC
and
GNC
can be readily used to bootstrap
STRIDE
. Particularly, when
RANSAC
and
GNC
succeed in finding the globally optimal solution (which happens frequently in the low-outlier regime),
STRIDE
serves to certify global optimality. Otherwise, when fast heuristics converge to local minima,
STRIDE
detects suboptimality and escapes such minima.

(IV) Evaluation on six geometric perception problems (Section
VI
)
.
Our last contribution is to apply our framework and solver to six perception problems: single and multiple rotation averaging, point cloud and mesh registration, absolute pose estimation, and category-level object perception. With extensive experiments on synthetic and real datasets, we demonstrate (i) our sparse SDP relaxation is exact in the presence of up to
\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\%
–
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers, (ii) while
still being far from real-time,
STRIDE
is up to 100 times faster than existing SDP solvers on medium-scale problems, and is the only solver than can solve large-scale SDPs with hundreds of thousands of constraints to high accuracy, (iii)
STRIDE
safeguards existing fast heuristics,
i.e.
, it certifies global optimality if the heuristic estimates are already optimal, or detects and escapes local minima otherwise.
We showcase real examples of
STRIDE

certifiably
performing scan matching on
3DMatch

[
117
]
, mesh registration on
HomebrewedDB

[
59
]
, satellite pose estimation on
SPEED

[
87
]
, and vehicle pose and shape estimation on
ApolloScape

[
101
]
.

Novelty with respect to
[
109
,
112
]
. This paper extends and unifies the contributions presented in our previous conference papers
[
109
,
112
]
. More in detail, we expand on
[
109
]
by
(i) showing that other robust costs (beyond TLS) can be rephrased as POPs,
(ii) providing a more extensive comparison between (and discussion about) Lasserre’s hierarchy and the proposed sparse relaxations,
(iii) going beyond certification (in this paper we propose a
solver
, rather than a certification approach),
(iv) considering a broader set of applications.
We also extend
[
112
]
, which introduced
STRIDE
, by
(i) generalizing
STRIDE
to work on multi-block SDPs arising from the proposed relaxations,
(ii) tailoring
STRIDE
to use fast heuristics (
e.g.
,
RANSAC
or
GNC
) as a warmstart, and
(iii) testing
STRIDE
on a broader range of problems.

We remark that the main goal of this paper is
not
to produce a method that outperforms problem-specific state-of-the-art algorithms in terms of robustness or efficiency. Our key contribution is instead to show that a broad
class of robust estimation problems in geometric perception can be solved to certifiable optimality in
polynomial time (despite their hardness), and lay out a scalable framework to build SDP relaxations, that we believe —with further advancement of SDP solvers— will eventually run in real time.

II
Preliminaries

This section reviews key facts about multi-block semidefinite programming
[
95
]
(Section
II-A
),
and provides an introduction to polynomial optimization and Lasserre’s semidefinite relaxation hierarchy
[
65
]
(Section
II-B
).
While somewhat mathematically dense, these preliminaries are designed as a pragmatic introduction for the non-expert reader.

II-A

Semidefinite Programming

A
multi-block
semidefinite programming (SDP) problem
is an optimization problem in the following
primal
form
[
95
]
:

\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}}\left\{\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891\ {\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}\right\}\mathchar 314

(P)

where the variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29036}\delimiter 84054785
is a collection of
\mathchar 29036
square matrices (the “blocks”) with
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29033}}}
for
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036
(conveniently ordered such that
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\dots\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29036}
);
the domain
\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}\triangleq\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 8706\dots\mathchar 8706\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29036}}
restricts the matrices to be symmetric.
The objective is a linear combination of the matrices in
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
,
i.e.
,
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\right\delimiter 0\triangleq\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29036}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 0
(for given matrices
{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29033}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036
).
The problem includes independent linear constraints
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}
on
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
, where:

{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\triangleq\left\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29036}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 0\,\mathchar 24635\,\dots\,\mathchar 24635\,\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29036}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 0\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}

(1)

for given matrices
{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29034}\!\mathchar 12850\!\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29033}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036
and
\mathchar 29034\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037
,
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\!\mathchar 12850\!{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
is a given vector.
Finally, the constraint
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\!\mathchar 12850\!{\cal\mathchar 29003}
enforces that each matrix in
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
is positive semidefinite
(

SDP solvers
.
The most robust approach for solving SDP (

), IPMs can solve the SDP to arbitrary accuracy,
i.e.
,
\mathchar 28945_{\max}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28962
for
\mathchar 28962
arbitrarily small, with a typical per-iteration complexity
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
.
2
2

2

{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
for spectral decomposition of dense primal and dual iterates
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\delimiter 84054785
,
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785

If each linear constraint only involves a small number of blocks (

[

.
3
3

3

“Dualization” switches the primal-dual data structure in numerical solvers (
e.g.
, writing the dual (

P
) such that
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}
is represented as an unconstrained cone, or difference of two nonnegative cones, with dimension
\mathchar 29037
)
[
71
]
. When sparsity exists, dualization can lead to better numerical performance.
Nevertheless, such sparsity is not always present and generally IPMs cannot solve large-scale problems on an ordinary workstation.

First-order methods based on ADMM and Augmented Lagrangian,
e.g.
,
CDCS

[
121
]
, and
SDPNAL+

[
116
]
, can handle large-scale problems but exhibit slow convergence, and hence can only obtain solutions of moderate accuracy.

For single-block problems (
\mathchar 29036\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
) with low-rank solutions (
i.e.
,
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12828\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}
) and
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
, the Burer-Monteiro (B-M) low-rank factorization method
[

,
22
]
is preferable. Section
I
mentioned the success of SDP relaxations in solving
outlier-free
perception problems.
This success is attributed to the following facts: (a) most of the SDPs arising in outlier-free estimation have
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, and can be solved by IPMs in less than one second; (b) although some SDPs (
e.g.
,
[
82
]
) can have
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, they can be efficiently solved by B-M because the optimal solution is low-rank and
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12825\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}

[
81
]
.

Challenges
. Unfortunately,
none
of the existing solvers can solve the SDPs presented in this paper to a desired accuracy. In particular, our SDPs have
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
but
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
as large as a few millions, rendering IPMs and B-M factorization inapplicable. Moreover, our SDPs admit rank-one optimal solutions and are necessarily degenerate
[
6
]
(loosely speaking, degeneracy is a property that often leads to slower convergence in SDP solvers and prevents the application of B-M). Our previous work
[
112
]
shows that first-order methods perform poorly on degenerate problems.

II-B

Polynomial Optimization and Lasserre’s Hierarchy

Polynomial optimization
. Given
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}\,\mathchar 24635\,\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28722}\,\mathchar 24635\,\ldots\,\mathchar 24635\,\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}
,
a
monomial
in
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
is a product of
\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}
’s with
nonnegative
integer exponents,
i.e.
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{{\bm{\mathchar 28939}}}\triangleq\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 29028}}
for
{\bm{\mathchar 28939}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29028}

(for instance
\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28725}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 28723}
is a monomial). The sum of the exponents,
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28939}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28721}
, or
\left\delimiter 0{\mathbf{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28939}}\right\delimiter 0

of the monomial (
e.g.
, the monomial
\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28725}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 28723}
has degree
\mathchar 28726
).

A real
polynomial

\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785
is a finite sum of monomials with real coefficients.
We shorthand
\mathchar 29040
in place of
\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785
when the variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
is clear.
The degree of a polynomial
\mathchar 29040
, denoted by
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\right\delimiter 84054785
, is the

degree of its monomials.
The ring of real polynomials is denoted by
\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779
. A standard polynomial optimization problem (POP) reads

\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}\triangleq\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}}\left\{\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\ \middle\ \begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}\displaystyle\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29032}\\
\displaystyle\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}\right\}\mathchar 24891

(POP)

where
\mathchar 29040\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779
. Problem (

Lasserre’s hierarchy
. We now give a simplified (and somewhat less conventional) introduction to Lasserre’s hierarchy that is sufficient for understanding our paper.
For a comprehensive treatment, we refer the reader to

]
.

We define

. For example, if

(6)

By construction,
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28948}}_{\mathchar 8235}
is positive semidefinite and has
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721

is simply
\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948}

(these monomials typically appear multiple times in

Therefore, a key fact is that

\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0

The key idea of Lasserre’s hierarchy is to (i) rewrite (
POP
) using the moment matrix
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}
,
(ii) relax the (non-convex) rank-1 constraint on
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}
, and
(iii) add redundant constraints that are trivially satisfied in (
POP
);
as we show below, this leads to a
convex
semidefinite program.

(i) Rewriting (
POP
) using
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}
.
We pick a positive integer
\mathchar 28948\!\mathchar 12850\!\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 8235}
(the
order
of the relaxation) such that
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12821\max\{\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\right\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 24891\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29032}}\right\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 24891\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}}\right\delimiter 84054785\!\}\mathchar 314
(this way we can express both objective function and constraints using

For instance, we can rewrite the objective and the equality constraints as:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\!\!\!\!\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} objective}}\mathchar 12346&\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\\

\begin{array}[]{ll}\!\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} equality constraints}}\mathchar 12346&\!\!\!\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29041}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891\;\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29032}\\

(8)

for suitable matrices
{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 28721}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29041}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}

Note that using
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}
is already a relaxation since we are no longer enforcing the entries of

to be monomials

(ii) Relaxing the (non-convex) rank-
\mathchar 28721
constraint on
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}

At the previous point we noticed we can rewrite objective and constraints in (

functions of
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}
still belongs to the set of positive-semidefinite rank-1 matrices, which is a non-convex set

\begin{array}[]{ll}\!\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} moment matrix}}\mathchar 12346&{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\\
\end{array}

(9)

(iii) Adding redundant constraints
. Since we have relaxed (

) by re-parametrizing it in
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}

and dropping the rank constraint, the final step to obtain Lasserre’s relaxation consists in adding extra constraints to make the relaxation tighter.
First of all, we observe that there are multiple repeated entries in the moment matrix (
e.g.
, in (
6
), the entry
\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28722}

appears 4 times in the matrix). Therefore, we can enforce these entries to be the same. In general, this leads to

\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 12349\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28948}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721

where
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\triangleq{{\mathchar 29038\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\over\mathchar 28722}}
is the dimension of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} moment constraints}}\mathchar 12346&\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891\\
&\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891\\
&\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28948}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 24891\end{array}

(10)

where
{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}
is all-zero except
\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721

\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721

Second, we can also add

\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8705\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948

\begin{array}[]{ll}\hskip-8.53581pt\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} (redundant) equality constraints}}\mathchar 12346\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29041}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891\\
\quad\quad\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29032}\mathchar 24891\ \ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\underline{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948\mathchar 8704\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785}\end{array}

(11)

for suitable
{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29041}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29034}

Since the first entry of
\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948\mathchar 8704\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785}

Finally, we observe that if
\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720

{\bm{\mathchar 29005}}

\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29005}}\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720

Since we can represent any polynomial of order up to

Following steps (i)-(iii) above, it is straightforward to obtain the following (convex) semidefinite program:

\hskip-11.38109pt\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 12349\!\!\displaystyle\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}}\delimiter 84054785}\left\{\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 12349\!{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\!\mathchar 12823\!\mathchar 28720\right\}\!\mathchar 24891\!\!\!

(LAS)

where the variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}}\delimiter 84054785
is a collection of positive-semidefinite matrices (
cf.
(
9
) and (

), and we shorthand
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}}
for notation convenience),
the objective is the one given in (

{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}

collect all the constraints in (

Problem (

) can be readily formulated as a multi-block SDP in the primal form (
P
), which matches
the data format used by common SDP solvers.
Problem (
LAS
) is commonly known as the
dense
Lasserre’s relaxation because a fully dense monomial basis
\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}
is used to build the moment matrix
[
65
]
. One can solve the relaxation for different choices of
\mathchar 28948
, leading to a
hierarchy
of convex relaxations.

While we presented Lasserre’s hierarchy in a somewhat procedural way, the importance of the hierarchy lies in its stunning theoretical properties, that we review below.

Theorem 2

(Lasserre’s Hierarchy
[
65
,
67
,
76
]
)
.

Let
\mathchar 8704\mathchar 561\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 561
be the optimum of (
POP
) and
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
(resp.
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
) be the optimum (resp. one optimizer) of (
LAS
), assume (
POP
) satisfies the Archimedeanness condition (a stronger form of compactness,
cf.

[
20
, Definition 3.137]
), then

(i)

(lower bound and convergence)
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
converges to
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
from below as
\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561
, and convergence occurs at a finite
\mathchar 28948
under suitable technical conditions
[
76
]
;

(ii)

(rank-one solutions) if
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
at some finite
\mathchar 28948
, then for every global minimizer
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}
of (
POP
),

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}\triangleq\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
is optimal for (
LAS
), and every rank-one optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
of (
LAS
) can be written as
\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
for some
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}
that is optimal for (
POP
);

(iii)

(optimality certificate) if
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
at some finite
\mathchar 28948

\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}

Theorem
2
states that (
LAS
) provides a hierarchy of lower bounds for (
POP
).
When the relaxation is exact (
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
), global minimizers of (
POP
) correspond to rank-one solutions of (
LAS
). Moreover, after solving the convex SDP (

), one can check the rank of the optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28948}
to obtain a

of global optimality. In practice, rank computation can be subject to numerical inaccuracies, and we introduce a continuous metric for evaluating the exactness of the relaxation in Section

(
cf.

III
Outlier-Robust Estimation as POP

(a) TLS

(b) MC

(c) GM

(d) TB

(e) L1

(f) Huber

(g) Adaptive

Fig. 1:
Estimation using common robust cost functions can be equivalently reformulated as polynomial optimization problems (
cf.
Proposition
4
).

In this section, we consider a general formulation of estimation with robust cost functions. We show that, for seven popular robust costs, this formulation can be recast as a (
POP
). We conclude the section by showcasing the resulting formulation on six perception problems.

Outlier-robust estimation
. Given a set of
\mathchar 29006
measurements
{\cal\mathchar 29018}\mathchar 12349\{{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}
(
e.g.
, 2D image keypoints, 3D point clouds, relative poses), we consider the problem of using
{\cal\mathchar 29018}
to estimate an unknown geometric model
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12818{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}
(
e.g.
, camera poses, rigid transformations, 3D shapes, robot trajectory), despite the fact that the measurement set
{\cal\mathchar 29018}
may contain a large amount of
outliers
. Building on standard M-estimation
[
74
,
92
]
, we perform outlier-robust estimation by solving the following optimization problem

\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12818{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}}\ \ \mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28960\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(Robust)

where
\mathchar 29042\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
is a (scalar)
residual
function that measures the mismatch between
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}
(
e.g.
, Euclidean distances, pose errors),
\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
(set by the user) is the
maximum admissible residual
for a measurement to be considered as an
inlier
(or minimum residual to be an outlier),
\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
is a
robust
cost function that penalizes outliers much
less
than inliers to prevent outliers from contaminating the estimate. We include a
regularization
term
\mathchar 28960\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785
in (
Robust
),
to keep full generality: as we will see in the examples below, a regularizer is often added to high-dimensional estimation problems
to ensure the solution is unique and well-behaved.
We make the following assumption on problem (
Robust
).

Assumption 3

(Polynomial Residual, Constraint, and Regularization)
.

In (
Robust
), assume (i)
\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960
are polynomials; (ii) the constraint
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}
can be described by finitely many polynomial equalities and inequalities,
i.e.
,
{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12349\{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29032}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}\}
.

Assumption
3
is the prerequisite for applying the machinery of semidefinite relaxation for POP in Section
II-B
. These assumptions are often mild in geometric perception problems, a point that will become clearer when we introduce the six examples later in this section
(
cf.
Proposition
7
)
.

Now the only component of (
Robust
) that may prevent it from being a POP is the robust cost
\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
. In
outlier-free
estimation,
\mathchar 28954\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}
is chosen as the
least squares
cost and (
Robust
) is immediately in the form of (
POP
). However, in outlier-robust estimation,
\mathchar 28954
is typically not a polynomial. For instance, let us consider the
truncated least squares
(TLS) cost, which will be extensively used in this paper:

\mathchar 28954_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29004\mathchar 29011}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\triangleq\min\left\{{{\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\over\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\right\}\mathchar 12349\begin{cases}{{\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\over\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}}}&\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29042\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\\
\mathchar 28721&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\mathchar 24891

(14)

The TLS cost (
14
) is apparently not a polynomial, and it is not even a
smooth
function (
cf.
Fig.
1
(a)).

Reformulation as POP
. To build intuition, we now show that (
Robust
) with the TLS cost (
14
) can be reformulated as a POP; then we generalize this conclusion to other cost functions in Proposition
4
.
The key observation is that, for any
\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}
,
\min\{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026\}\mathchar 12817\min_{\mathchar 28946\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\}}{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28946\over\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 29025\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28946\over\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 29026
which allows recasting (
Robust
) with
\mathchar 28954\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28954_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29004\mathchar 29011}}
as

\min_{\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12818{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}\mathchar 24891\\
{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8710\mathchar 28721\}^{\mathchar 29006}\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}}\ \mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\over\mathchar 28722}}{{\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\over\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\over\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28960\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(TLS)

where each binary variable
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\}
decides whether the
\mathchar 29033
-th measurement
{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}
is an inlier (
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721
) or an outlier (
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721
). By recalling that
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\}\mathchar 12844\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
, we see that problem (

) is an instance of (
POP
), with the decision variables now being
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29006}}
. The next proposition states that, the reformulation above can be generalized to a broader set of robust cost functions.

.

4

4

\{\{{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}\}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}

Hence, category-level perception estimates the pose
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8706{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}

{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 8235}

\mathchar 29042\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069
,

, the Euclidean distance between the transformed 3D keypoint detections and the nonnegative combination of the shape bases. We include
\mathchar 28960\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28949\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}
as a regularization for the shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
, as in
[

]
. Again, we enforce
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 28723}_{\mathchar 29012}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 29012}

Proposition 7

(Polynomial Expressibility)
.

Examples
1
-
6
satisfy Assumption
3
. Precisely, (i)
\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}
and
\mathchar 28960
(if
\mathchar 28960\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 28720
) are quadratic polynomials (
i.e.
,
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
); (ii) the constraint set
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
can be described by polynomial equalities
\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}
’s and inequalities
\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}
’s with degree up to 2 (
i.e.
,
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28722
).

While we postpone the proof to Supplementary Material, we observe that the key insights behind the proof are simple but powerful: (i) rigid body transformations can be expressed as linear functions (
e.g.
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}
for a given point
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}
),
(ii) squared residuals
\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}
(and our regularizer
\mathchar 28960
) are commonly squared L2 norms, that can be written as quadratic functions,
and (iii) the set of poses and rotations can be described by quadratic (in-)equality constraints, a fact already used in,
e.g.
,
[
94
,
30
,
25
,
108
]
.

Proposition
4
and
7
together establish that outlier-robust geometric perception (
Robust
) with TLS, MC, GM, TB, L1, Huber and Adaptive costs (Fig.
1
), when applied to Examples
1
-
6
(Fig.
2
), are instances of (
POP
). The expert reader will also recognize other geometric perception problems that satisfy Assumption
3
, including 2D-2D relative pose estimation
[
25
]
, triangulation
[
5
]
, rotation search (Wahba problem)
[
107
]
,
pose graph optimization
[
82
]
, among others. Although the bundle adjustment problem
[
3
]
cannot be written as a POP using the geometric reprojection error, adopting the point-to-line error can put bundle adjustment in the form of a POP
[
85
]
. Nevertheless, bundle adjustment typically involves too many variables (
e.g.
, hundreds of camera poses and hundreds of thousands of 3D points) to be practically solvable using existing semidefinite relaxations.

For the rest of the paper, we will focus on designing certifiable algorithms and semidefinite relaxations for (
Robust
) with the (
TLS
) cost function. However, semidefinite relaxations proposed in Section
IV
can be extended to the other costs in Proposition
4
, and we leave that exercise to the interested reader. We end this section with a remark about why we prefer the TLS cost over the others in Proposition
4
.

Remark 8

(Preference for TLS)
.

(i) Compared to GM, TB, L1 and Huber, which still penalize outliers, TLS completely discards outliers. Consequently, TLS can often achieve better robustness to outliers
[
105
,
92
]
. (ii) MC also completely discards outliers, but it does not select a model to minimize the inlier residuals. Therefore, there can be an infinite number of solutions to problem (
Robust
) with equal cost (number of outliers). (iii) The adaptive cost typically leads to POPs with high-degree polynomials, which requires a large
\mathchar 28948
from the relaxation hierarchy and results in SDPs that are intractable. (iv) TLS can be shown as a maximum likelihood estimator, when the inliers have a Gaussian distribution and the outliers are
uniformly distributed, see
[
8
, Proposition 5]
.

IV
Sparse Semidefinite Relaxation

In the previous section, we showed how to rephrase the TLS cost as a nonconvex polynomial optimization in
\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\triangleq\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\,\mathchar 24635\,{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29006}}
. The goal of this section is to design algorithms that can solve (
TLS
) to certifiable global optimality.

Can we just use Lasserre’s hierarchy?

Before introducing our sparse semidefinite relaxation, let us attempt to apply the dense Lasserre’s hierarchy (
LAS
)
to (
TLS
). We know that the objective in (
TLS
) has degree
\mathchar 28723
,
5
5

5

The residuals
\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
are quadratic from Proposition
7
,
hence the terms
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
in the objective of (

) become cubic.
thus
\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
is needed for (

). In fact, as we have shown in
[

) at
\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
is empirically exact (on small problem instances). However, as we can see from Examples

, the SDP resulting from the dense relaxation (

\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28726\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28729\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28721

Sparse semidefinite relaxation (SSR)
. Now we present a semidefinite relaxation that is much more scalable than (
LAS
). Note that the fundamental reason why (

) leads to an intractable SDP is the use of the
dense
monomial basis
\delimiter 67482370\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}
for building the moment matrix
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28948}

\delimiter 67482370\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28948}
is necessary when the polynomials
\mathchar 29040\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}
contain all monomials up to degree
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28948
, in practice
\mathchar 29040\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}
are almost always
sparse
(
i.e.

Theorem 9

(Sparse Semidefinite Relaxation for (
TLS
))
.

Denote by
\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84054785
the objective function of (

), by
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
the optimum of (

\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
(resp.
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}

), we have

(i)

(lower bound)
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
;

(ii)

(rank-one solutions) if
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
, then for each global minimizer
\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
of (

), its rank-one lifting
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\delimiter 69640972}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
is optimal for (

), and every rank-one optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}
of (

) can be written as
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
for some

that is optimal for (

);

(iii)

(optimality certificate) if
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
, then
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
.

Theorem
9
states that (
SSR
) is a relaxation for (
TLS
) and solving the convex SDP (
SSR
) can provide a certificate for the exactness of the relaxation if the rank of the optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}
equals one. In practice, rank computation can be subject to numerical inaccuracies (
e.g.
, it can be difficult to decide if the relaxation is exact when the second largest eigenvalue is, say
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28723}
). Therefore, we now introduce a continuous metric for evaluating the exactness of the relaxation (that also applies to the dense relaxation (
LAS
)).

Relative suboptimality
.
Assume
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}
is an optimal solution of (
SSR
) and let
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}
be the eigenvector corresponding to the maximum eigenvalue of
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}
. If the maximum eigenvalue has multiplicity larger than one, then choose
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}
as any of the eigenvectors corresponding to the maximum eigenvalue.
Define the rounding function

We end this section with a remark about how to exploit sparsity while preserving exactness of the relaxation.

Remark 10

(Exploiting Sparsity)
.

(i) A sparse relaxation can be exact only when the dense relaxation (
LAS
) is exact. Therefore, we believe it is good practice to first obtain an empirically exact relaxation using the dense hierarchy (
LAS
) at certain
\mathchar 28948
(as we have done in
[
109
]
with extensive experimental validation), and then try to find a sparse monomial basis at that
\mathchar 28948
. (ii) When the dense relaxation is exact, it is nontrivial to decide if a sparse relaxation will be exact without empirical evaluation. For example, replacing (
15
) with
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\,\mathchar 24635\,{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84267779
is also a sparse relaxation —the corresponding moment matrix includes all monomials in
(i)
-
(iii)
— but it is far from being exact. (iii) Parallel to our work
[
109
]
,
[
99
]
has presented a methodology,
TSSOS
, to systematically exploit term sparsity for general POPs.
However,
TSSOS
tends to find a larger monomial basis when compared to problem-specific techniques such as (
SSR
) in this paper. For Example
5
with
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
measurements, the dense monomial basis has dimension
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28726
, our sparse basis (
15
) has dimension
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28723
, but
TSSOS
with “maximal chordal extension” finds a sparse basis that has dimension
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28726
and is a strict superset of (
15
).

V
STRIDE
: Scalable SDP Solver

The sparse relaxation (
SSR
) leads to an SDP that can still have
\mathchar 29037
as large as hundreds of thousands when
\mathchar 29006
is large (
cf.
Fig.
3
). Therefore, with IPMs such as
MOSEK
, the scale at which (
SSR
) can be solved is still quite limited (recall IPMs can typically handle
\mathchar 29037
up to
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
). This section presents
STRIDE
(
SpecTrahedral pRojected gradIent Descent along vErtices
),
an SDP solver that goes far beyond IPMs and enables solving (
SSR
) on problems of
moderate but realistic size.

Intuition
. The key insight behind
STRIDE
comes from Theorem
9
(ii): assuming the relaxation (
SSR
) is exact, then the SDP (
P
) admits
rank-one
optimal solutions
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\delimiter 69640972}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, where
\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
corresponds to the global minimizer of (
TLS
).
Therefore,
STRIDE
tries to move between rank-one matrices in the feasible set of the SDP (these are the
vertices
of the spectrahedron
[
20
]
), searching for a globally optimal solution.
More in detail,

STRIDE
employs a globally convergent
projected gradient descent
(PGD) method as the backbone for solving the convex SDP (
SSR
), but blends
short
PGD steps with
long
rank-one steps generated by fast NLP algorithms on the POP (
TLS
).
Intuitively, the long rank-one steps circumvent the slow convergence of PGD, while the PGD backbone allows escaping local minima where the NLP algorithm can be stuck in.

With this insight, we now develop the details of
STRIDE
.

Short PGD step
.
The backbone of
STRIDE
implements a PGD for solving the primal SDP (
P
). Given an initial point
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}
, the
\mathchar 29035
-th (
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720
) iteration of PGD performs

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(PGD)

for a given constant
\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, where
\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}}
denotes the metric projection onto the spectrahedron
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}\triangleq\{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\}
(
i.e.
, the feasible set of (
P
)). In words, the (
PGD
) step first moves along the direction of the negative gradient for some step size
\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}
(recall the objective of (
P
) is
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\right\delimiter 0
with a constant gradient
{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}
), and then projects the new point
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}
onto the feasible set
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
.
It is well known that (
PGD
) guarantees to converge to an optimal solution of (
P
), provided that
\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720
(see
[
57
,
16
,
17
]
). In Supplementary Material, we show the Lagrangian dual of the projection subproblem in (
PGD
)
can be reformulated as a
smooth unconstrained optimization
, which allows solving (
PGD
) for large-scale problems using a limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) algorithm. For this reason, in (
32
) we also output the dual optimal solution.

Long rank-one step
.
The issue with (
PGD
) is that the convergence can be slow, particularly when the optimal
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
is rank-one and degenerate (as in (
SSR
)). Here we propose to exploit the low-rankness of
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
and accelerate the convergence by generating long rank-one steps. Towards this goal, calling
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\delimiter 84054785
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}_{\delimiter 69640972}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}}_{\mathchar 8235}
as the first block in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
(
i.e.
, the moment matrix), we compute a potentially better rank-one iterate via three steps:

(i)

(Rounding)
. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\delimiter 69640972}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\delimiter 69640972}
with
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\dots\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}}
in nonincreasing order. Compute
\mathchar 29042

\mathchar 29042
eigenvectors

\displaystyle\delimiter 67273472\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\texttt{rounding}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891

(29)

where the function

is defined as in (
27
).

(ii)

(Local search)
. Apply a local search method for (
TLS
) using NLP with initial point chosen as
\delimiter 67273472\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29042
. Denoting the solution of each local search as
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349

(iii)

(Lifting)
. Perform a rank-one lifting of the best local solution

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349

\displaystyle\!\!\!\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\delimiter 69640972}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}}\mathchar 24891\dots\delimiter 84054785_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}}\mathchar 24891

(31c)

where
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}
are computed by
evaluating
the moment and localizing matrices at
\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
.

Taking the right step
.
Now we are given two candidates for the next iteration, namely the short PGD step
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
(generated by computing the projection of
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}
onto
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
) and the long rank-one step
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
(obtained by rounding, local search, and lifting). Which one should we choose to be the next iterate
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
such that the entire sequence
\{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\}
is globally convergent? The answer to this question is quite natural –we accept
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
if and only if it attains a strictly lower cost than
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
(
cf.
eq. (
33
)).

Given
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 8706{\cal\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 8706{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
, a tolerance
\texttt{tol}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, an integer
\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84267779
, a constant
\mathchar 28943\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, a nondecreasing sequence
\{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720\}
. Perform the following steps for
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots
.

(Short PGD step)
.
Solve the projection problem with initial point
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785

\displaystyle\delimiter 67273472\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(32)

using the L-BFGS algorithm in
[
112
, Section 4]
.

(Long rank-one step)
. Compute a candidate rank-one iterate
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
from (
29
)-(
31
).

(Update primal variable)
. Update
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
as

\hskip-11.38109pt{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\begin{cases}\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}&{\rm\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29030}\;\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}\displaystyle\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28943\\
\displaystyle\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}\\
