(33)

(Check convergence)
.
Compute KKT residuals at
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
from (
5
), if
\mathchar 28945_{\max}\mathchar 12604\texttt{tol}
, then output
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
. Otherwise, go to Step
1
.

Algorithm 1

STRIDE

The full
STRIDE
algorithm is presented in Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 11

(Global Convergence)
.

Suppose the Slater condition for (
P
) holds and
\{\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\}
is generated by
STRIDE
, then
\{\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\}
converges to
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
, where
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
is the optimum of (
P
).

While we provide the proof in Supplementary Material, the intuition is that
eq. (
33
) ensures the rank-one “strides” are accepted only if they strictly decrease the objective value.
Therefore, either the last rank-one point is already optimal, or —if it is suboptimal— it still provides an improved reinitialization for (
PGD
) to globally converge to the optimal
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
.
Note that the PGD backbone allows
STRIDE
to converge even when the optimal solution has rank higher than one. In
[
112
]
, we show it is also possible to
accelerate
and
generalize
the (
PGD
) backbone using
proximal gradient methods
.

Although
STRIDE
is a globally convergent algorithm for solving the primal SDP (
P
), the initial guess
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
can have a significant impact on its convergence speed. The next remark states that existing fast heuristics for robust perception can be readily incorporated into
STRIDE
.

Remark 12

(Fast Heuristics and Certification)
.

Existing fast heuristics for robust estimation, such as graduated non-convexity (
GNC
)
[
105
,
18
]
and
RANSAC

[
47
]
, can typically return the
globally optimal
solution to (
TLS
) when the measurement set
{\cal\mathchar 29018}
contains a low or medium portion of outliers (
e.g.
, below
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
). Therefore, we use
GNC
or
RANSAC
to generate an initial guess for the SDP relaxation (
SSR
). Formally, calling
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}\delimiter 84054785
the candidate solution obtained by solving (
TLS
) using
GNC
or
RANSAC
, we generate
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}
(for
STRIDE
) by applying the lifting procedure in (
31
) to
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}\delimiter 84054785
. Notably, when
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}\delimiter 84054785
is already globally optimal to (
TLS
) (hence
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}
is an optimizer of (
SSR
) as long as the relaxation is exact),
STRIDE
only finds a
certificate of optimality
for
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}\delimiter 84054785
by performing one step of (
PGD
) (
cf.
(
32
) in Algorithm
1
).

Fast heuristics provide a good
primal
initialization for
STRIDE
. However, little information is known about how to obtain a good
dual
initialization. In Supplementary Material, we describe a dual initialization procedure that exploits
correlative sparsity

[
98
]
and leverages a fast first-order algorithm called
semi-proximal ADMM
(also known as
ADMM+
)
[
91
]
. We also give more implementation details about how to use Riemannian optimization to perform local search.

VI
Experiments

In this section, we test the sparse relaxation (
SSR
) and the SDP solver
STRIDE
on Examples
1
-
6
using both synthetic and real data (we defer the results for Example
4
mesh registration to Supplementary Material due to space constraints). The goal of our experiments is not to claim state-of-the-art efficiency or robustness (
e.g.
, against problem-specific implementations), but rather to show that (
SSR
) and
STRIDE
, for the first time, provide a general framework to solve large-scale nonconvex outlier-robust perception problems to certifiable global optimality within reasonable computation time. We believe with the advancement of SDP solvers, our framework will eventually run in real time.

Baselines
. We use two state-of-the-art SDP solvers,
MOSEK

[
9
]
and
SDPNAL+

[
116
]
, as baseline solvers to compare against
STRIDE
. We omit
MOSEK
whenever the SDP becomes too large to be solved by
MOSEK
(
i.e.
, when
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
). We use default settings for both
MOSEK
and
SDPNAL+
.

STRIDE
’s settings
. In Algorithm
1
, we choose
\texttt{tol}\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28726}
,
\mathchar 29042\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28723
,
\mathchar 28943\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722}
,
\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\!\mathchar 12349\!\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568\mathchar 29035
, and run it for a maximum of
\mathchar 28725
iterations. As described in Remark
12
, we use
GNC
or
RANSAC
to initialize the primal variable, and
ADMM+
to initialize the dual variable. The local search is performed using
Manopt
with a trust region solver. Details about local search and
ADMM+
can be found in Supplementary Material.

Evaluation metrics
. Let
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}}\delimiter 84054785
be the solution for (
SSR
) returned by an SDP solver
and
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}\delimiter 84054785
be the corresponding rounded solution for (
TLS
).
We evaluate the performance of the solver using four metrics:
(i) the estimation errors of
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}
compared to the groundtruth, whenever the groundtruth is available;
(ii) SDP solution quality, using the maximum KKT residual
\mathchar 28945_{\max}
from (
5
);
(iii) certified suboptimality, using the
rounding
procedure in (
27
) and the relative suboptimality measure
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
in (
28
) (we deem a rounded solution as globally optimal if
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28723}
);
(iv) solver CPU time in seconds. For simulation experiments, statistics are computed over
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
Monte Carlo runs per setup.

Hardware
. Experiments are performed on a Linux PC with 12-core Intel i9-7920X CPU@2.90GHz and 128GB RAM.

Single Rotation Averaging

(a)
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
,
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
,
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28726

(b)

,

Fig. 4:
Single Rotation Averaging (Example
1
).

Setup
. At each Monte Carlo run, we first randomly generate a groundtruth 3D rotation
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}
; then inliers are generated by
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}_{\mathchar 28962}
,
where the inlier noise
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}_{\mathchar 28962}
is generated by randomly sampling a rotation axis and a rotation angle
\mathchar 28962\mathchar 12824{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28955^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
with
\mathchar 28955\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725^{\mathchar 8718}
; outliers are arbitrary random rotations. We test two setups with
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
. At
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
, we sweep the outlier ratio from
\mathchar 28720\%
to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
, while at
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, we sweep the outlier ratio up to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%
.

Results
. Fig.
4
(a)-(b) plot the evaluation metrics for
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, respectively. We make the following observations. (i) Our sparse relaxation (
SSR
) is exact with up to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
and up to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%
outliers when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
(the suboptimality
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
is below
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28723}
for all test runs). (ii) For
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
,
STRIDE
solves the SDP to an accuracy that is comparable to
MOSEK
(
cf.
the
\mathchar 28945_{\max}
plot), but is about
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
(and up to
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720
) times faster (
cf.
the time plot). (iii) For
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
,
MOSEK
cannot run. While
SDPNAL+
still runs, its accuracy is at least five orders of magnitude worse than
STRIDE
(
cf.
the
\mathchar 28945_{\max}
plot, where
STRIDE
attains
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728}
accuracy, but
SDPNAL+
only attains
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28723}
accuracy), and its runtime is about
\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28720
times slower than
STRIDE
. (iv)
STRIDE
safeguards
GNC
. While
GNC
is used to initialize
STRIDE
,
STRIDE
can
certify
the global optimality of
GNC
and escapes the local minima of
GNC
(
e.g.
, at
\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate in the rotation error plot, while
GNC
fails many times, the solution of
STRIDE
is always correct and optimal). (v) When the outlier rate is too high, global optimality not necessarily implies a correct estimation (in the sense of being close to the groundtruth). For example, at
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
,
STRIDE
and
MOSEK
both obtain certifiable optimality (
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
), but the rotation error can be quite large (about
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8718}
). Similarly, at
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%
outlier rate when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, the optimal estimate obtained by
STRIDE
also has large rotation errors.
For further discussion about this point,
we refer the reader to the notion of
estimation contract
in
[
113
]
, which
ties the number of inliers to the accuracy of the optimal solution of (
TLS
) w.r.t. the groundtruth, and
reports estimation contracts for a 3D registration problem.

. We test 2D multiple rotation averaging in a SLAM setting, where a robot traverses a trajectory following a 2D grid pattern (

. Fig.

for the more challenging

. Fig.

\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%

and

can obtain a certifiably optimal solution, except that

\mathchar 28725

\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%

7

7

Consistent with
[
109
]
, we empirically noticed that the relaxation breaks earlier when fewer measurements are available. We remark that the formulation considered in this paper is more challenging that the rotation-only version in
[
107
]
, which remains tight at
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers.

However,
STRIDE
is about
\mathchar 28725
times faster than

.
SDPNAL+
completely fails in this problem. (ii) When

\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers and
STRIDE
is the only solver that can certify exactness. At
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate,
STRIDE
certified the global optimality for
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28727
runs, while failed to do so for
\mathchar 28723
runs. (iii)
STRIDE
can certify the success of
GNC
and escape local minima when
GNC
fails (
e.g.
, at
\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%

and at
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
).

i.e.
,
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8718}\delimiter 84054785

is defined as
{\cal\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29025}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28723}\,\mathchar 24635\,\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84267779

Results
. Fig.

\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\%

\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate, even if

\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%

VI-F

Summary and Discussion

Table
II
summarizes the timing results of
STRIDE
, compared with
MOSEK
, for all six problems. We make a few comments. (i)
STRIDE
is able to solve problems far beyond the reach of
MOSEK
(in fact, the SDPs solved in this paper are among the largest in all semidefinite programming literature). (ii) When fast heuristics converge to the globally optimal solution,
STRIDE
just needs to perform optimality certification and can be
\mathchar 28722
-
\mathchar 28725
times faster (
cf.

STRIDE
(Certify) vs.
STRIDE
(Escape)). (iii) For problems of similar sizes (in terms of
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}
and
\mathchar 29037
), the speed of
STRIDE
can be
application-dependent
(
e.g.
,
STRIDE
is much faster in single rotation averaging than in other applications). This suggests that relaxations of different applications lead to SDP problems of
drastically different geometry
. Understanding the geometry and leveraging new tools to further speedup the computation is an exciting research venue. For example, it could be promising to use data-driven methods to “learn” the geometry of different problems to generate high-quality initializations.

TABLE II:
Average timing of
STRIDE
and
MOSEK
(in seconds). We omit
SDPNAL+
because it failed to solve most of the problems. “
\mathchar 8707\mathchar 8707
” indicates
MOSEK
out of memory. “N/A” indicates
GNC
or
RANSAC
succeeded for all instances and there was no data for
STRIDE
escaping local minima.

VII
Related Work

We review related works on outlier-free and outlier-robust geometric perception, while we refer the interested reader to
[
116
,
99
]
for recent progress in semidefinite programming and semidefinite relaxations.

Outlier-free geometric perception
algorithms can be divided into
minimal solvers
and
non-minimal solvers
. Minimal solvers assume
noiseless
measurements (
i.e.
,
\mathchar 29042\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568\;\mathchar 29033
in (
Robust
)) and use the minimum number of measurements necessary to estimate
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
, which typically
leads to solving a system of polynomial equations
[
63
]
.
Non-minimal solvers account for measurement noise and estimate
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
via nonlinear least squares (NLS),
i.e.
,
\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}
in (
Robust
).
While in rare cases NLS can be solved in closed form
[
53
]
or by solving the polynomial equations arising from the first-order optimality conditions
[
62
]
, in general
they lead to
nonconvex problems and are attacked using local solvers
[
84
]

or exponential-time methods (
e.g.
,
Branch and Bound

[
78
]
).

Certifiable algorithms
for outlier-free perception have recently emerged as an approach to compute globally optimal
NLS
solutions in polynomial time.
These algorithms
relax
the NLS minimization
into a convex optimization, using Lasserre’s hierarchy of semidefinite relaxations for
polynomial optimizations

[
67
,
58
]
. By solving the SDP resulting from the convex relaxations,
certifiable algorithms compute global solutions to NLS problems and provide a certificate of optimality,
which usually depends on the rank of the SDP solution or the duality gap.
Empirically tight convex relaxations have been discovered in pose graph optimization
[
29
,
82
]
, rotation averaging
[
45
,
48
]
, triangulation
[
5
]
, 3D registration
[
24
,
73
,
31
]
, absolute pose estimation
[
4
]
, relative pose estimation
[
25
,
119
]
, hand-eye calibration
[
52
]
and shape and pose estimation from 2D or 3D landmarks
[
108
,
88
]
. More recently, theoretical analysis of when and why the relaxations are tight is also emerging
[
30
,
5
,
45
,
82
,
39
,
119
,
31
,
43
,
55
,
46
]
.
Tight relaxations also enable
optimality certification (
i.e.
, checking if a given solution is optimal), which —in outlier-free perception— can sometimes be performed in closed form
[
29
,
45
,
49
,
22
,
26
,
81
,
55
]
. Despite being certifiably optimal, these solvers assume all measurements are inliers (
i.e.
, have small noise), which rarely occurs in practice, and hence give poor estimates even in the presence of a single outlier.

Outlier-robust geometric perception
algorithms can be divided into
fast heuristics
and
globally optimal solvers
. Two general frameworks for designing fast heuristics are
RANSAC

[
47
]
and
graduated non-convexity
(
GNC
)
[
18
,
105
,
8
]
.
RANSAC
robustifies minimal solvers and acts as a fast heuristics to solve
consensus maximization

[
35
]
, while
GNC
robustifies non-minimal solvers and acts as a fast heuristics to solve
M-estimation
(
i.e.
, using a robust cost function
\mathchar 28954
in (
Robust
)). Local optimization is also a popular fast heuristics
[
84
,
2
]
for the case where an initial guess is available. Approximate but deterministic algorithms have also been designed to solve consensus maximization
[
68
]
.
On the other hand, globally optimal solvers are typically designed using Branch and Bound
[
15
,
79
,
56
,
115
,
80
,
69
,
34
,
70
]
.

Certifiable outlier-robust algorithms
relax problem (
Robust
) with a robust cost into a tight convex optimization.
While certain robust costs, such as L1
[
100
]
and Huber
[
27
]
, are already convex, they have low breakdown points (
i.e.
, they can be compromised by a single outlier
[
74
]
). Problem-specific certifiably robust algorithms have been proposed
to deal with high-breakdown-point formulations, such as the TLS cost
[
106
,
107
,
64
]
.

VIII
Conclusions

We presented the first general and scalable framework to design certifiable algorithms for outlier-robust geometric perception. We first showed that estimation with several common robust cost functions can be reformulated as polynomial optimization problems. We then designed a semidefinite relaxation scheme that exploits the sparsity of outlier-robust estimation to generate SDPs of much smaller sizes while maintaining empirical exactness. Finally, we proposed a robust and scalable SDP solver,
STRIDE
, that can solve the sparse relaxations to unprecedented scale and accuracy. We tested our framework on six geometric perception applications using both synthetic and real data, demonstrating its robustness, scalability, and capability to safeguard existing fast heuristics for robust estimation.

\blacksquare

A2
Proof of Proposition
7

Proof.

(i) can be proved by inspection: all the
\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}
and
\mathchar 28960
in Examples
1
-
6
are squared norms of
affine
(degree-one) polynomials in
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
, and are naturally quadratic.

To show (ii), we note that the
\mathchar 29041
-dimensional ball
{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29041}_{\mathchar 29012}
can be described by a single quadratic inequality

A4
Relative Suboptimality

This section is concerned with the computation of a formally correct suboptimality gap
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}

for a given estimate (which we use as a performance metric in our experiments), whose validity is not hindered by potential numerical inaccuracies in the solution of the SDP relaxation (
SSR
).

In Theorem
9
and (
28
), we stated that, by solving the sparse SDP relaxation (
SSR
) to global optimality with optimizer
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
and associated optimum
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
, one can round from
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
a feasible solution
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
to the original (
TLS
) problem with associated cost
\widehat{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
. Then, a measure of suboptimality for the rounded solution
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
can be computed as follows (also in (
28
)):

\displaystyle\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\triangleq{{\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathchar 29040}\right\delimiter 69640972\over\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 8235\left\delimiter 69640972\widehat{\mathchar 29040}\right\delimiter 69640972}}\mathchar 314

(A24)

It is apparent that
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
implies the relaxation (
SSR
) is exact and the rounded solution
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
is indeed globally optimal for (
TLS
) (recall
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12820\widehat{\mathchar 29040}
by construction, where
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
is the unknown optimum of the nonconvex (
TLS
) problem).

However, the caveat in computing the relative suboptimality as in (
A24
) is that, although it is almost always possible to compute a rounded solution
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}}_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
with cost
\widehat{\mathchar 29040}
(provided that the feasible set
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
of (
TLS
) is simple to project, as in our examples), it can be quite challenging to obtain
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
(which acts as a valid lower bound for
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
)
to machine precision
, since
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
is computed by numerically solving the SDP (
SSR
), which may still lead to small inaccuracies. Moreover, as shown in the experimental section of the main text, first-order solvers such as
SDPNAL+
typically cannot solve the SDP to even moderate accuracy (with reasonable amount of iterations), in which case
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
in not attained.

Here we describe a procedure to compute a valid lower bound for
\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 8511}
, from any approximate solution
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 8706{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 8706\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}
of the SDP (
SSR
), without requiring it to be an optimal solution satisfying the KKT conditions (
3
). In fact, as we will show soon, only
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
is needed to compute a valid lower bound.

Bounded trace
. Let us first shows that, each block of the primal variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
in (
SSR
) has a bounded trace, when (
SSR
) is applied to all six Examples
1
-
6
. Towards this goal, let us first observe that the variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}
has a bounded norm,
i.e.
, there exists
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
such that
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}
. For example, in Example

,
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
has
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0\sqrt{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 28720}
; in Example

,
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\delimiter 84054785
with
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 28723}_{\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29044}}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29027}}
has
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12820\sqrt{\mathchar 28723\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29044}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29027}^{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 28720}
, where
\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29044}
and
\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29027}
are the upper bounds for the norm of the translation and the norm of the shape parameters, respectively.

Now recall that the primal variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
has
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}
blocks, with the first block being the moment matrix and the other
\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031}
blocks being localizing matrices. With the observation that
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}
, we can bound the trace of the moment matrix
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\delimiter 69640972}
(

(

A6
Solving the Projection Subproblem

In this section, we describe how to solve the projection subproblem in
STRIDE
(
cf.
(
PGD
) and (
32
) in Algorithm
1
). In particular, we show that the dual problem of (
PGD
) admits an unconstrained formulation, which allows developing a scalable algorithm based on limited-memory BFGS.

Recall that the projection step (
32
) of
STRIDE
seeks to compute the projection of a given point onto the spectrahedron
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}\mathchar 12349\{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}\}
.
Formally, given a point
{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}
, the projection problem seeks the closest point in
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
w.r.t.
{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}

\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}}\left\{{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\ \middle\ {\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}\right\}\mathchar 314

(A33)

Since
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
is the intersection of two convex sets, namely the hyperplane defined by
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}
and the (product of) positive semidefinite cone
{\cal\mathchar 29003}
, a natural idea is to apply Dykstra’s projection algorithm (see
e.g.
,
[
40
]
) to generate an approximate solution by alternating the projection onto the hyperplane and the projection onto the semidefinite cone, both of which are easy to compute. However, Dykstra’s projection is known to have slow convergence and it may take too many iterations until a satisfactory projection is found. As a result,
instead of solving (
A33
) directly, we consider its dual problem

\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}}\left\{{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 8235{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\right\delimiter 0\ \middle\ {\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}\right\}\mathchar 24891

(A34)

where we have ignored the constant term
\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}
and converted “
\max
” to “
\min
” by changing the sign of the objective.
The KKT conditions for the pair (
A33
) and (
A34
) are:

\hskip-8.53581pt{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891\ {\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\mathchar 24891\ {\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 24891\ \left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 314\!\!

(A35)

An unconstrained formulation
. Now we introduce a key observation that allows us to further simplify the dual (
A34
). Fixing the unconstrained
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}
, problem (
A34
) can be seen as finding the closest
{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}
w.r.t. the matrix
\mathchar 8704{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}
, and hence admits a closed-form solution

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 29003}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(A36)

As a result, after inserting (
A36
), problem (
A34
) is equivalent to

\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}}\ \ \mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 29003}}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891

(A37)

with the gradient of
\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785
given as

\displaystyle\mathchar 626\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 29003}}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 314

(A38)

Thus, if
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 8511}
is an optimal solution for problem (
A37
), we can recover
{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 8511}
from (
A36
), and
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
from the KKT conditions (
A35
):

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29018}}\mathchar 314

(A39)

Formulating the dual problem as the unconstrained problem (
A37
) has appeared multiple times in
[
120
,
72
]
.

Now that (
A37
) is a
smooth unconstrained convex
problem in
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
, plenty of efficient algorithms are available, such as (accelerated) gradient descend
[
75
]
, nonlinear conjugate gradient
[
42
]
, quasi-Newton methods
[
77
]
and the semismooth Newton method
[
120
]
. In this paper, we apply the celebrated limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method, see for example
[
77
, Algorithm 7.5]
. L-BFGS is easy to implement, can handle very large unconstrained optimization problems due to its low memory consumption, and is typically “the algorithm of choice” for large-scale problems
[
77
, Chapter 7]
. Empirically, we observed that L-BFGS is efficient and robust for various applications. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that demonstrates the effectiveness of L-BFGS, or in general quasi-Newton methods, in solving large-scale and degenerate SDPs.

A7
Proof of Theorem
11

Proof.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 12349\{\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\delimiter 84054785}\}
be the sequence of all the
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}
that have been accepted due to (
33
), where
\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\delimiter 84054785
returns the iteration index of the
\mathchar 29033
-th element in
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
. If
{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 571
, then
STRIDE
reduces to (
PGD
) and is globally convergent. If
{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 571
, then we claim that
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
must be finite. Note that, for any two consecutive elements
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\delimiter 84054785}
and
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
in
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820

\displaystyle\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721{}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28943

(A40)

\displaystyle\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28943\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28943\mathchar 24891

where the first inequality is due to (
33
), the second inequality is due to (
32
) and the fact that projected gradient descent must strictly decrease the objective value when optimality has not been achieved
[
17
, Proposition 3.4.1]
, and the last inequality holds because
\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\delimiter 84054785
. Eq. (
A40
) states that the objective value must decrease by at least
\mathchar 28943
along each element of
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
. Therefore, we have
\mathchar 29030_{\min}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29030_{\max}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 28943
, where
\mathchar 29030_{\min}
and
\mathchar 29030_{\max}
are the minimum and maximum objective values along
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
. Hence
\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 69640972
must be finite, otherwise
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8511}
is unbounded below, contradicting Slater’s condition and strong duality. Let
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 84054785}
be the last element of
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
, then
STRIDE
reduces to (
PGD
) with a new initial point at
\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}}^{\mathchar 29035\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 84054785}
and is globally convergent.

\blacksquare

A8
Implementation Details for
STRIDE

In Section
V
, we presented the
STRIDE
algorithm and proved its global convergence. We noted that the initial point
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
could have a significant impact on the convergence speed of
STRIDE
. Therefore, in
STRIDE
we use existing fast heuristics (
GNC
,
RANSAC
) to generate a
primal
initial guess (
cf.
Remark
12
). In this section, we describe how to generate a
dual
initial guess (Section
A8-A
), and how to use Riemannian optimization for local search (Section
A8-B
).

A8-A

Dual Warmstart

We propose to use a combination of two techniques to generate a good dual initial point
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
. Section
A8-A1
describes a method to relax the (
TLS
) problem by exploiting correlative sparsity. Although such a relaxation is not tight, we show that its solution can be used to warmstart
STRIDE
. In Section
A8-A2
, we present a fast first-order algorithm to refine both the primal and the dual initializations.

A8-A
1
Bootstrapping via Correlative Sparsity

The (
TLS
) problem has another special property called
correlative sparsity

[
98
,
97
,
66
]
, which, loosely speaking, refers to the property that there exists a partition of the variables
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84054785
into a union of smaller groups, such that (i) each constraint of (
TLS
) involves only one group of the variables, and (ii) the objective of (
TLS
) can be decomposed into terms that each involves only one group of the variables (
cf.

[
66
, Assumption 2]
).
Particularly, we observe that the objective polynomial, denoted by
\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84054785
, can be expressed as a sum of
\mathchar 29006
polynomials:

\displaystyle\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}\underbrace{\left\delimiter 67273472{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\over\mathchar 28722}}{{\mathchar 29042^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29050}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\over\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\over\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29006}}\mathchar 28960\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29040_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 24891

(A41)

where each
\mathchar 29040_{\mathchar 29033}
is a polynomial that only involves
\widetilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}}_{\mathchar 29033}\triangleq\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\,\mathchar 24635\,\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}}

This correlative sparsity allows us to design an SDP relaxation for (

where the linear constraints simply enforce each entry of

to be a linear combination of entries in
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}}
, and
\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29033}}
is the number of inequality constraints in
{\cal\mathchar 29000}_{\mathchar 29033}

(iv) Adding overlapping constraints
. The extra step that needs to be performed when there are multiple moment matrices is to add constraints that enforce

to be the same. Clearly, from (

\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\,\mathchar 24635\,{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 84267779\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 84267779

for all
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006

\begin{array}[]{ll}\hskip-8.53581pt\!\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5} overlapping constraints}}\mathchar 12346&\!\!\!\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29039\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29040}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28721}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29039\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29040}}\mathchar 24891\\
&\!\!\!\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006\mathchar 24891\end{array}

(A56)

where

A8-A
2
Semi-proximal ADMM

After obtaining
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}
from primal heuristics such as
GNC

[
105
]
or
RANSAC

[
47
]
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}
from solving (
CSSR
) and performing the assembly procedure in Section
A8-A1
, we use the semi-proximal alternating direction method of multipliers (
ADMM+
) proposed in
[
91
]
to refine both the primal and the dual initializations
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
. The full
ADMM+
algorithm, for solving a standard SDP (
P
)-(
D
), is presented in Algorithm
2
. As we can see, at each iteration of
ADMM+
, the major computation involves solving a linear system (
cf.
(
A73
) and (
A75
)) and performing a projection onto the product of positive semidefinite cones
{\cal\mathchar 29003}
(
cf.
(
A74b
)). Since
{\cal\mathchar 28993}
is typically sparse in our examples, Cholesky factorization of
{\cal\mathchar 28993}{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}
can be done efficiently and needs to be performed only once.
ADMM+
is a globally convergent algorithm for solving the SDP (
P
)-(
D
) and the interested reader can refer to
[
91
]
for a detailed study. Notably,
[
91
]
shows that
ADMM+
is typically
\mathchar 28722
to
\mathchar 28723
times faster than a conventional ADMM. In our implementation, we use the function
admmplus
in
SDPNAL+

[
116
]
to refine
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
and warmstart
STRIDE
. Although one can directly pass
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
to
STRIDE
, empirically we found it is beneficial to refine
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785
using
ADMM+
because the refined initial points will have higher quality that promotes the convergence of
STRIDE
. In our experiments, we run
ADMM+
for a maximum of
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
iterations, or until
\max\{\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29028}\}
is below a threshold (
e.g.
,
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28726}
).

Given
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29011}}^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}

\mathchar 28955\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, and
\mathchar 28941\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785

.

A8-B

Local Search and Nonlinear Programming

Recall the local search step (
30a
) applies a nonlinear programming (NLP) algorithm to solve the (
TLS
) problem given an initial point. Since (
TLS
) is a polynomial optimization, it is straightforward to implement NLP using
fmincon
in Matlab. However, here we show that it is possible to exploit the smooth manifold structure of (
TLS
) and solve it more efficiently with Riemannian optimization
[
1
]
(
e.g.
, using
Manopt

[
21
]
). First, we can model the vector of binary variables
{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}
as an
oblique manifold
of size
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721
(an oblique manifold contains matrices with unit-norm rows). Second, from Examples
1
-
6
, we know the geometric model
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
contains 2D and 3D rotations, which are both smooth manifolds. However,
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
can also contain translation
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}
and shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
that do not live on smooth manifolds. Fortunately, we can drop some constraints so that they both live on smooth manifolds. For example, in Examples
3
,
4
, and
6
, we can relax
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 28723}_{\mathchar 29012}
to
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}
, with the rationale that when the SDP iterate
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 29035}
is close to optimal,
\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29012
should be naturally satisfied (from rounding (
29
)) even without explicit constraint. Similarly, we relax
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 28723}_{\mathchar 29012}\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 28995}_{\mathchar 28939}
in Example
5
to
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}
, and relax
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 29012}
in Example
6
to
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29003}_{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 8235}
(matrices with strictly positive entries live on a smooth manifold). Note that these modifications will not affect the global convergence of
STRIDE
because (
33
) will reject the NLP solution if it violates the constraints that have been dropped.

Setup
.
We first simulate a random mesh by sampling a set of
\mathchar 29006
3D planes
\{{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}
, where
{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}
is the unit normal of the plane (by sampling a random 3D direction) and
{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12824{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
is an arbitrary point on the plane. We then generate a random point on each plane via
{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29047}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8706{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}
, where
{\bm{\mathchar 29047}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12824{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
is a random point and “
\mathchar 8706
” denotes the vector cross product. After this, we generate a random groundtruth transformation
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8718}\delimiter 84054785
, and transform
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
to be
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8718}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 28962}}_{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29033}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29045}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\texttt{normalize}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 28962}}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
, if
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29045}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
is an inlier, where
{\bm{\mathchar 28962}}_{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28962}}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12824{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721^{\mathchar 28722}{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
are random Gaussian noise, and
\texttt{normalize}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 84054785\triangleq{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\delimiter 68408078\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}\right\delimiter 0
normalizes a vector to have unit norm. If
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29045}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
is an outlier, then
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}
is a random 3D point and
{\bm{\mathchar 29045}}_{\mathchar 29033}
is a random 3D direction. Given the mesh
\{{\bm{\mathchar 29041}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\delimiter 69640972}}_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}
and the noisy point cloud with normals
\{{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29045}}_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}
, we seek the best transformation
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
to
align the point cloud to the mesh
using the residual defined in Example
4
. After
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
is found, its
inverse
transformation is used to compute the estimation errors w.r.t.
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8718}\delimiter 84054785
(recall
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8718}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8718}\delimiter 84054785
is generated to align the mesh to the point cloud). We test
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
with increasing outlier rates.

Results
. Fig.
A1
(a)-(b) plots the evaluation metrics for
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, respectively. The results are mostly the same as point cloud registration in Fig.
6
(a)-(b), except that when
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
, the relaxation is not always tight at
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
and
\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rates (from the
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
plot of
MOSEK
we see one inexact run at
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
and three inexact runs at
\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%
).

Mesh registration with
TeddyBear
. We perform mesh registration using the
TeddyBear
mesh model from the
HomebrewedDB
dataset
[
59
]
. From the
TeddyBear
mesh, we generate a noisy point cloud by densely sampling points on each face of the mesh with additive Gaussian noise, and transform the point cloud using a random rigid transformation. We use the
pcnormals
function in Matlab to estimate surface normals for each point in the point cloud. We then randomly sample
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720
point-to-face correspondences with outliers, and use
STRIDE
to estimate the rigid transformation. Fig.
A1
(c-1) shows an instance with
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\%
outliers, where
GNC
successfully returns the globally optimal solution and
STRIDE
computes a certificate of optimality (
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728}
). Fig.
A1
(c-2) shows an instance with
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%
outliers, where
GNC
converges to a suboptimal solution but
STRIDE
escapes the local minimum and finds the globally optimal solution with a certificate of optimality (
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28727}
).

A9-B

Robustness of the TLS Estimator

Here we show that the accuracy of the TLS
estimator increases with the number of inliers and is comparable
with a least-squares solution computed from the inliers only.
We perform an experiment in single rotation averaging with outlier rate fixed at
\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%
but number of measurements
\mathchar 29006
increased from
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
to
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
. At each
\mathchar 29006
, we perform 20 random simulations and compute the rotation estimation error w.r.t. groundtruth. Fig.
A2
(c) shows that all TLS solutions are certified as globally optimal. Fig.
A2
(a) shows that, as
\mathchar 29006
increases and the number of inliers increases, the estimation error in general decreases (both in terms of the average estimation error and the quantiles as shown by the boxplot). This demonstrates the empirical robustness of the TLS estimator against outliers and its capability in exploiting information from the inliers.

Using the same experimental setup, we compare the rotation error between the TLS estimator and the least squares (LS) estimator (after running TLS, we discard the measurements deemed as outliers by TLS and run LS on the remaining inliers). Fig.
A2
(b) shows that the TLS estimator is exactly the same as the LS estimator after discarding outliers (up to numerical inaccuracies, the rotation errors are shown in degrees). This demonstrates that the outliers do not affect the TLS solution, and the TLS estimator is truly robust against outliers.

(a) TLS vs. Groundtruth

(b) TLS vs. LS

(c) Certified Suboptimality

Fig. A2:
Rotation estimation error under increasing number of measurements for single rotation averaging with
\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\%
fixed outlier rate (thus increasing number of inliers). (a) Rotation error between TLS estimate and groundtruth rotation. (b) Rotation error between TLS estimate and least squares (LS) estimate. (c) All TLS solutions are certified as optimal. Rotation errors shown in degrees.

A9-C

Scaling the Noise Bound

We perform an experiment to investigate how the optimal solutions change when the noise bound
\mathchar 28940
is varied, using the single rotation averaging problem with
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720
measurements and
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate.
Fig.
A3
plots the rotation estimation error and certified suboptimality w.r.t. different scaling on the original noise bound
\mathchar 28940
(all the measurements are the same at each random simulation, only the noise bound
\mathchar 28940
is chosen differently). We can see that (1) there is a wide range of
\mathchar 28940
that leads to certifiably optimal and accurate estimation, and (2) when the noise bound
\mathchar 28940
is slightly perturbed (
e.g.
, decreased to
\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
or increased to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\%
), the optimal solution remains the same for most problem instances, as shown by the similar boxplots in Fig.
A3
(a) at horizontal locations
\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729
,
\mathchar 28721
, and
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721
(in fact,
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725
out of the
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
runs have exactly the same solutions).

(a) Rotation estimation error

(b) Certified suboptimality

Fig. A3:
(a) Rotation estimation error and (b) certified suboptimality w.r.t. scaling on the noise bound
\mathchar 28940
in single rotation averaging with
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\%
outlier rate.

Heng Yang
is a Ph.D. candidate in the Laboratory for
Information & Decision Systems at the
Massachusetts Institute of Technology. He has obtained a B.S. degree in Mechanical
Engineering (with honors) from the Tsinghua University, Beijing, China, in 2015; and an S.M. degree
in Mechanical Engineering from MIT in 2017.
His research interests include large-scale convex optimization, semidefinite relaxation, robust estimation, and machine learning, applied to computer vision, robotics, and trustworthy autonomy.
Heng Yang is a recipient of the Best Paper Award in Robot Vision at the 2020 IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), a Best Paper Award Honorable Mention from the 2020 IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters (RA-L), and a Best Paper Award Finalist at the 2021 Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS) conference. He is a Class of 2021 RSS Pioneer.

Luca Carlone

is the Leonardo Career Development Associate Professor in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, and a Principal Investigator in the Laboratory for Information & Decision Systems (LIDS).
He has obtained a B.S. degree in mechatronics from the Polytechnic University of Turin, Italy, in 2006;
an S.M. degree in mechatronics from the Polytechnic University of Turin, Italy, in 2008;
an S.M. degree in automation engineering from the Polytechnic University of Milan, Italy, in 2008;
and a Ph.D. degree in robotics also from the Polytechnic University of Turin in 2012.
He joined LIDS as a postdoctoral associate (2015) and later as a Research Scientist (2016), after spending two years as a postdoctoral fellow at the Georgia Institute of Technology (2013-2015).
His research interests include nonlinear estimation, numerical and distributed optimization, and probabilistic inference, applied to sensing, perception, and decision-making in single and multi-robot systems. His work includes seminal results on certifiably correct algorithms for localization and mapping, as well as approaches for visual-inertial navigation and distributed mapping.
He is a recipient of the Best Student Paper Award at IROS 2021, the Best Paper Award in Robot Vision at ICRA 2020, a 2020 Honorable Mention from the IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters, a Track Best Paper award at the 2021 IEEE Aerospace Conference, the 2017 Transactions on Robotics King-Sun Fu Memorial Best Paper Award, the Best Paper Award at WAFR 2016, the Best Student Paper Award at the 2018 Symposium on VLSI Circuits, and he was best paper finalist at RSS 2015 and RSS 2021. He is also a recipient of the AIAA Aeronautics and Astronautics Advising Award (2022), the NSF CAREER Award (2021), the RSS Early Career Award (2020), the Google Daydream (2019) and the Amazon Research Award (2020, 2022), and the MIT AeroAstro Vickie Kerrebrock Faculty Award (2020).
